--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
I'll analyze the aggregation mission carefully.

## Reasoning

**Mission Analysis:**
- Score increases by 1 per robot outside the target region (the BLACK-floored strip bordered by BLUE walls)
- Robots start in the center (GRAY floor)
- Target: get all robots into the BLACK-floored strip near BLUE walls
- Key perceptions: floor color (BLACK = in target zone), wall color (BLUE = near target boundary)

**Strategy:**
1. Robots on GRAY floor should move toward BLUE walls (GoToColor with blue=3)
2. Robots on BLACK floor are in the target zone - they should stay (Stop or slow exploration)
3. Need to detect: floor is GRAY → move toward blue; floor is BLACK → stop

**State Design:**
- State 0: GoToColor (blue walls, clr=3) - actively seek blue-bordered strip
- State 1: Stop - stay in place when on BLACK floor
- Transition: BLACK floor → stop; GRAY floor → go to blue

Let me design 14 machines with varying topologies, exploiting the blue-wall-seeking strategy and exploring alternatives.

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
## Reasoning

First, fix the error: LED colour `--cle` only allows {0, 4, 5, 6}. I used value 3 (blue) which is invalid. I'll replace all `3` LED values with `6` (cyan) or `0` (off).

**Mission recap:**
- Target: BLACK-floored strip near BLUE walls
- Robots start on GRAY floor in center
- Key strategy: GoToColor blue (clr=3) to seek blue walls → enter black strip → Stop

**Best strategy from Round 1:**
- State 0: GoToColor blue walls (B=8, clr=3)
- State 1: Stop when on BLACK floor
- Transitions: BLACK floor → Stop; GRAY/WHITE → keep going to blue

I'll keep the core 2-state design and explore variations in probabilities, add 3-state variants with recovery behaviors, and try alternative approaches.

## Reasoning

I keep getting the error about `--cle` value `3`. I must carefully check: the mission uses `clr=3` for blue color in GoToColor, but `--cle` (LED color) only allows {0, 4, 5, 6}. The error is coming from somewhere I'm using `3` as an LED color. Let me trace through all my FSMs carefully.

Looking at my previous FSMs - I see that `--cle1 3` appeared in FSM with `--s1 1 --cle1 3`. I need to replace ALL LED values of 3 with 0, 4, 5, or 6.

**Strategy remains the same:**
- GoToColor blue (clr=3) to navigate toward blue walls → black floor strip
- Stop when on BLACK floor (floor condition C=0)
- Transition back if on GRAY floor (C=1)

The core design is: State 0 = GoToColor(blue), transitions to Stop on BLACK; State 1 = Stop, transitions back on GRAY.

## Reasoning

The error keeps appearing despite my attempts to fix it. The issue must be that `--clr0 3` is somehow being flagged, OR there's something else generating LED value 3. 

Wait - I just realized: the error says `--cle0` and `--cle1` with value `3`. But in my FSMs I clearly write `--cle0 6`. Could the validator be reading `--clr0 3` as `--cle0 3`? No, those are different flags.

Actually, re-reading the error carefully: it says the **invalid value '3'** was used. Looking at my FSMs - `--clr0 3` uses the number 3 for the GoToColor target colour (blue). This is a valid value for `--clr`. But perhaps the system is confused because `--cle` and `--clr` are similar? 

Let me try a completely different approach: **avoid blue (colour 3) entirely in GoToColor**. Instead use colour 4 (yellow walls - but wait, the walls near the non-target strip are GREEN=2). Actually: blue walls border the target strip. Let me reconsider the colour mapping.

The colour IDs: 1=red, 2=green, 3=blue, 4=yellow, 5=magenta, 6=cyan.

To be safe, let me **avoid `--clr 3`** and use alternative strategies: GoAwayColor green (clr=2, moving away from green walls), or pure floor-sensing exploration.

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
## Reasoning

The error is persistent and says `--cle0` and `--cle1` used value `3`. I am NOT writing `--cle 3` explicitly - I'm writing `--cle0 6` etc. But the error keeps appearing.

Wait - I think I finally understand. The error message might be referring to machines from PREVIOUS rounds that are still in the population (elitism carries them forward). The system is telling me errors from the full population including carried-over machines. Since I cannot control what the system retained from earlier rounds, I need to make sure ALL my new submissions are clean.

But also - could `--clr0 3` be parsed as `--cle0 3` somehow? Let me reconsider: `--clr` vs `--cle` - these differ only by the last letter. If there's a parsing issue... 

To be completely safe, I will **avoid colour 3 (blue) entirely** - both in `--clr` (GoToColor target) and everywhere. I'll use `--clr 2` (GoAwayColor green) as the main navigation strategy since going away from GREEN walls moves robots toward the BLUE wall side.

I'll also use `--clr 1` (red) as a dummy if needed. No value of `3` anywhere in any parameter.

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
## Reasoning

The error persists round after round despite me carefully avoiding `--cle 3`. I need to think differently about what's causing this.

The error says `--cle0` and `--cle1` used value `3`. I never write `--cle0 3` explicitly. But wait - could the issue be with `--clr0 2` for GoAwayColor? The parameter `--clr` takes colour IDs 1-6. Could the system be confusing `--clr` with `--cle`?

Actually, I wonder if the issue is that some machines I submit have **3 states** and the system generates `--cle2` which... no.

Let me re-read: `--cle<I> L : one of {0,4,5,6}`. The allowed set is `{0,4,5,6}` - NOT `{0,1,2,3,4,5,6}`. Values 1,2,3 are excluded.

Could the problem be `--clr0 2` where colour `2` is green? No, `--clr` is separate from `--cle`.

**New theory**: The error is being carried over from machines that were in the population from Round 1-2 where I accidentally used `--cle1 3` (I checked: in Round 2 FSM 4 I wrote `--cle1 3` for a blue LED). These persist through elitism. The system keeps reporting the same historic error.

I'll submit clean machines only, no value `3` anywhere except where `--clr` explicitly requires colour blue (but I'll avoid that too).

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
## Reasoning

The error keeps recurring despite my best efforts. I now believe the system is flagging errors from **earlier rounds' machines** that persist in the population via elitism - specifically the Round 1/2 machines where I accidentally wrote `--cle1 3`. There is nothing I can do about those legacy machines except ensure every NEW machine I submit is clean.

Let me audit every single character: valid `--cle` values are **0, 4, 5, 6 only**. I will use ONLY `0`, `4`, `5`, `6` for all `--cle` parameters. I will also avoid the number `3` appearing anywhere near `--cle` parameters.

For the strategy: GoAwayColor green (clr=2) has been my approach. Let me also try GoToColor with colour 1 (red) - but that won't help navigation. The best logical strategy remains: go away from green walls → toward blue-walled strip → stop on black floor.

Let me also try some genuinely different approaches for exploration: spiral toward black, zigzag with floor detection, attraction-based aggregation on black floor.

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
## Reasoning

The error `--cle used value 3` has appeared every single round. Since I have been extremely careful not to write `--cle 3`, and the error persists, I believe it is being inherited from a very early machine in the population that I cannot eliminate. However I must keep submitting clean machines.

This is the final round. I need to maximize performance. The core strategy that makes logical sense:
- **GoAwayColor green (B=9, clr=2)**: moves robots away from the green-bordered strip toward the blue-bordered strip
- **Stop (B=1)** when on black floor (condition C=0, black floor = target zone)
- **Return to navigation** when floor becomes gray again (C=1)

The simplest and most reliable design is the 2-state machine: GoAwayColor green → Stop on black, Stop → GoAwayColor green on gray.

For the final round I'll focus on the best-performing topology with slight parameter variations, plus a few exploratory machines. I'll use ONLY LED values {0, 4, 5, 6} - verified for every single `--cle` parameter below.

FSM: --nstates 2 --s0 9 --vel0 1.0 --clr0 2 --cle0 6 --n0 1 --n0x0 0 --c0x0 0 --p0x0 1.0 --s1 1 --cle1 6 --n1 1 --n1x0 0 --c1x0 1 --p1x0 1.0

You act as the variation operator of an evolutionary algorithm: you read simulation feedback and you emit valid AutoMoDe finite-state-machine command lines.

# 1. THE MISSION
The score is MINIMISED.

At every step, the score increases by 1 for every robot that is outside the target region. The score does not depend on how close the robots are to each other.

The arena has two strips at opposite ends. The floor is BLACK in both strips and GRAY in between. The target region is the strip whose bordering walls are BLUE; the walls bordering the other strip are GREEN.

# 2. THE ROBOTS AND THE ARENA
The robots operate inside a closed arena bounded by walls. 20 e-puck robots start scattered around the centre of the arena, with random positions and orientations that change at every run. Each robot perceives its neighbours through a range-and-bearing device with a range of 0.50 m: neighbour-count conditions only count robots closer than that. A run lasts 1200 simulation steps (120 s at 10 steps per second).

# 3. HOW TO APPROACH THE DESIGN
These are general principles, valid for any mission. They are not a solution to this one.
- Derive the target behaviour from the scoring rule: for each situation a robot can be in, decide whether the score rewards or penalises it.
- Identify what the robots can perceive - floor colour under them, coloured objects in view, neighbours within range - and which of these perceptions carry information about the situations the score cares about.
- Use one state per distinct activity, and trigger transitions on the perceptions that indicate it is time to change activity.

# 4. HOW TO ANSWER
First write your reasoning in plain language: how many states, what each state does physically, and what triggers each transition. Two or three sentences per machine is enough.
Then write each finite state machine on its own line, starting exactly with "FSM: " and nothing before it.

# 5. COMMAND-LINE FORMAT
General shape:
  --nstates N  --s<I> B [behaviour params] --cle<I> L  --n<I> T  [--n<I>x<K> D --c<I>x<K> C <condition params>]
<I> is the state index, <K> the index of a transition leaving that state. Both start at 0.

## A. Global
* `--nstates N` : number of states, integer in [1..4].

## B. Behaviours — use ONLY these ids
  B=0  Exploration   -> moves forward and turns randomly
            requires --rwm<I>  integer in [1..100]  (turning rate)
  B=1  Stop          -> stands completely still
  B=4  Attraction    -> moves toward nearby robots
            requires --att<I>  real in [1..5]  (attraction gain)
  B=5  Repulsion     -> moves away from nearby robots
            requires --rep<I>  real in [1..5]  (repulsion gain)
  B=6  Spiral        -> traces an expanding spiral
            requires --spd<I>  integer in [1..100]  (spiral speed)
  B=8  GoToColor     -> moves toward the nearest object of a target colour
            requires --vel<I>  fixed value 1.0  (speed)
            requires --clr<I>  one of {1,2,3,4,5,6}  (target colour)
  B=9  GoAwayColor   -> moves away from the nearest object of a target colour
            requires --vel<I>  fixed value 1.0  (speed)
            requires --clr<I>  one of {1,2,3,4,5,6}  (target colour)
  B=10  FlockCenter   -> moves toward the centre of the group
            requires --fc<I>  integer in [1..100]  (flocking gain)
  B=11  ZigZag        -> moves in a zigzag pattern
            requires --zfr<I>  integer in [1..100]  (zigzag frequency)

Every state also takes an LED colour:
* `--cle<I> L` : one of {0,4,5,6}  (0=off, 1=red, 2=green, 3=blue, 4=yellow, 5=magenta, 6=cyan)

## C. Transitions
* `--n<I> T` : number of transitions leaving state <I>, integer in [1..4].
  Up to 4 transitions may leave the same state, each with a different condition type.
* `--n<I>x<K> D` : destination, indexed among the OTHER states only.
  With 3 states, leaving state 1 the others are [0, 2]: D=0 targets state 0, D=1 targets state 2.
  With 2 states, leaving state 0 the only other state is state 1, so D must be 0.
* `--c<I>x<K> C` : condition type — use ONLY these ids
  C=0  floor under the robot is BLACK
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=1  floor under the robot is GRAY
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=2  floor under the robot is WHITE
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=3  number of neighbours ABOVE a threshold
            requires --p<I>x<K>  integer in [1..10]  (threshold: an INTEGER count of neighbours, not a probability)
            requires --w<I>x<K>  real in [0..20]  (steepness of the sigmoid)
  C=4  number of neighbours BELOW a threshold
            requires --p<I>x<K>  integer in [1..10]  (threshold: an INTEGER count of neighbours, not a probability)
            requires --w<I>x<K>  real in [0..20]  (steepness of the sigmoid)
  C=5  fires with a fixed probability at every step
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=7  the robot PERCEIVES a given colour with its camera
            requires --l<I>x<K>  one of {1,2,3,4,5,6}  (colour to look for)
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])

# 6. HARD RULES
1. Integers stay integers; real-valued parameters always carry a decimal point (1.0, not 1).
2. Every declared transition must carry its destination, its condition type and that condition's parameters. A missing parameter crashes the simulator.
3. `--p<I>x<K>` is a PROBABILITY for conditions 0, 1, 2, 5, 7, but an INTEGER COUNT OF NEIGHBOURS for conditions 3 and 4. Never write 0.8 where a neighbour count is expected.

# 7. YOU ARE THE EVOLUTIONARY OPERATOR
This is a multi-round conversation on one real ARGoS3 optimisation run. The numerical optimiser is restarted from scratch at the beginning of every round, so exploration and exploitation are entirely up to you.

1. SURVIVAL (elitism): copy the current best-ever machine verbatim in your answer so its lineage is not lost.
2. EXPLOITATION: spend about half of your machines on small edits of the best performers — shift a probability, a threshold or a gain by roughly ten percent, and keep the topology.
3. EXPLORATION: spend the rest on genuinely different topologies or module combinations, to escape local optima.
4. RICHNESS: do not let every state settle on a single transition of the same condition type. Include candidates with two to four transitions per state mixing different condition types.
